42	"Hell: Where the fire is not quenched and the worms do not die.█


Mark 9: 44: 'where their worm does not die, and the fire is never quenched.'


Isaiah 66: 24: 'for their worm shall not die, neither shall their fire be quenched.'


Both texts use exactly the same description to point to an irreversible fate reserved exclusively for the wicked. If it is claimed that the law and the prophets were not abolished, the question naturally follows: how do they intend to reconcile this with the claim that a righteous man descended to that place?


If you read further in Isaiah 66, those who are in hell are described as the men who rebelled against Yahweh. According to Matthew 5:17-18, Jesus did not rebel against Yahweh because he did not come to rebel against Yahweh's law. But wait a minute, why were we told that he rejected 'an eye for an eye' and preached turning the other cheek, which is like saying 'offer the other eye to the enemy' instead of applying the just law? That is not what someone who came to fulfill the law would say; only a rebel against Yahweh would say that.


But wait a minute... and who told us that narrative if not the empire that murdered the righteous man, breaking the law against killing innocent people (Exodus 20: 13)? And wait, on top of that, the official narrative of that empire tells us that its victim not only went to a place where only those who rebel against Yahweh go, but that he himself would have rebelled against that god Yahweh by accusing him of being the author of a narrative that contradicts the just commandment of 'an eye for an eye'.


Something does not add up. Here you will see why I think there are men who rebelled against Yahweh, not only by murdering the saints, but also by bearing false witness about them and their teachings...


Preaching to the dead is nonsense: they cannot hear. Descending into hell is absurd: that place does not exist. Jesus never descended into hell.


How could Jesus have descended into hell if, according to Revelation 20:12–15, that place only exists after the final judgment?


Isaiah 66: 24 describes that fate this way: 'for their worm shall not die, neither shall their fire be quenched.' Never means there is no rescue.


They claim that Jesus descended into hell based on 1 Peter 3:18–20 and Matthew 12: 40. In 1 Peter, it is stated that the righteous man died for the unrighteous and then went to preach to the spirits who sinned in the days of Noah. This idea does not hold up, because Proverbs 17: 15 says that God abhors both the one who justifies the wicked and the one who condemns the righteous, and Proverbs 29: 27 states that the righteous abhor the wicked.


Furthermore, why preach to the wicked? Daniel 12: 10 states that the wicked cannot follow the path of righteousness, and Revelation 9: 20 confirms that they do not repent even under punishment. According to 2 Peter 2: 5, God did not spare the ancient world, but preserved Noah, a preacher of righteousness. If Noah had already preached and the wicked were destroyed, it was because they did not listen. What sense does it make to insist on preaching a message of righteousness to those who have already proven themselves allergic to righteousness?


Luke 16: 26 speaks of a great chasm that no one can cross and describes people incapable of repenting, even if one of the dead were to speak to them.


According to Matthew 25: 41, hell is 'the eternal fire prepared for the devil and his angels', an eternal punishment reserved for the unrighteous, not the righteous.


Daniel 12: 10 says that only the righteous are cleansed of their sins. The righteous can repent; the wicked cannot. Psalms 118 declares: 'Yahweh chastened me, but he did not give me over to death… I will pass through the gates of righteousness, through which the righteous shall pass.'


Jesus alludes to this prophecy in the parable of the wicked tenants when he speaks about his return (Matthew 21:33–43). That return is inconsistent with returning in the same body, because punishment implies correction, and correction implies prior ignorance. That presupposes a new body.


Rome preached in Acts 1:1–11 that Jesus will return with the same body with which it claims he ascended into heaven, after rising from the dead and having been 'in the heart of the earth three days and three nights'. Even if that were true, there is a contradiction: on the third day, Jesus would still be there and, at the same time, would have risen from the dead. Rome took Hosea 6: 2 out of context, where the 'days' are an allusion to millennia and do not speak of the return to life of a single person, but of several. This is linked to Daniel 12: 2 and Psalms 90: 4.


If Jesus' religion was tied to the law and the prophets, and Rome did not respect his message, it is logical to conclude that it respected neither the law nor the prophets. That is why the contradictions in the texts that Rome eventually called the 'Old Testament' come as no surprise.


I will show you some examples: in Genesis 4: 15, the murderer is protected from the death penalty by God himself. But in Numbers 35: 33, the murderer is condemned by God's law. According to Ezekiel 33:18–20, the righteous can indeed become unrighteous, and the unrighteous can indeed become righteous. However, according to Daniel 12: 10, the righteous cannot become unrighteous, nor can the unrighteous become righteous. If the unrighteous could truly become righteous, Jesus' original message would not have been persecuted by anyone, but accepted by everyone.


The grand finale: The logic of conscious punishment and the true nature of eternal life:


If we speak of eternal punishment, we understand that eternal means having no end, and punishment implies pain. If we speak of eternal reward, its logical opposite implies pleasure and fulfillment without end.


Under this principle, is it possible for punishment to be punishment without pain? If the answer is no, then for pain to exist, there must be a conscious body that feels it; if they die and disappear, the punishment comes to an end because there is no one left to experience it. That is why the prophecy of Isaiah 66: 24 is entirely consistent: those bodies act as 'living dead'. The worms consume flesh that is never destroyed because it enters a process of perpetual regeneration, endlessly feeding the fire and the worms. It is a conscious agony of perpetual death — the 'second death' mentioned in Revelation — where not even the option of dying to find rest exists anymore.


And under that same logical symmetry, we evaluate the reward: just as a conscious body is required to feel the pain of punishment, a biological body is required to experience the reward.


When the writings of Daniel 12: 2 present 'eternal life' as a reward, the logic points inexorably toward a real biological life. Do you see any ethereal spirit or incorporeal soul carrying out biological processes? A spirit does not drink water. Yet Isaiah 65 states with material precision: 'My servants shall drink, but you shall be thirsty'. Eternal life is not an abstract entelechy; it is the glory of health and biological youth restored for all eternity, because the experience of bliss requires living, conscious matter.


Therefore, the equation closes inescapably:


For the wicked, agony has no end.


For the righteous, bliss has no end.


If death continued to operate, for the wicked it would mean the end of their agony, and for the righteous the end of their happiness. That is why the rational conclusion presented by these texts is that death itself must cease to exist, which is why both the prophecies of Isaiah and Revelation agree in marking the definitive end of death and casting it into the fire. https://gabriels.work/2026/10/30/como-pudo-descender-jesus-al-infierno-si-el-infierno-solo-existe-luego-del-juicio-final-y-si-se-supone-que-solo-los-malos-van-al-infierno/

Changing the form or giving sacred names to images does not change the act. Calling an image or statue by another name does not change the fact that it is an image or a statue. If you kneel to pay homage to it, lower your gaze before it, or burn incense to it, you do not please God: you please the idolatrous priest. If ancient Rome, which murdered the saints, continued doing this, there are no grounds to believe that it respected the message or that all the books of the Bible that emerged from its councils are free from its adulterations. I will point out one clue: you will never find a passage in the Gospels in which Jesus is shown describing what idols are and what idolatry is. Yet, according to the Bible's own texts, idolatry was clearly described by Isaiah and Habakkuk.


Isaiah described how an image is carved from a tree, before which a man bows down to plead with it (Isaiah 44). Today, we are told that, by doing exactly that, they are not committing idolatry because, in reality, they are praying to God. Is God deaf if a piece of wood is not used to speak to Him?… Cynicism is the main characteristic of every false prophet. Habakkuk also describes how mute images are useless and curses those who pray to them (Habakkuk 2:18–19).


The same Bible tells us that, because of his words, Jesus was mistaken for the ancient prophets (Matthew 16:13–14). If he was mistaken for the prophets, it is logical that his messages would align with theirs. But isn't it strange that there is not a single parable or message attributed to him that describes and condemns, so clearly, the worship of statues or images? I believe he did speak clearly about this, but Rome decided to remove that text because it made it uncomfortable.


It is also very strange that in Acts and other books of the New Testament, we do not see a clear description of what it means to worship images as idols. The only direct reference in the New Testament is in Revelation 9:20:


'And the rest of the men who were not killed by these plagues still did not repent of the works of their hands, nor did they stop worshipping demons and images of gold, silver, bronze, stone, and wood, which can neither see, nor hear, nor walk'.


Despite this, the hypocritical guide says that they know their images are not God and that they know they cannot hear, speak, feel, or walk, but that even if people kneel before them, they do not worship them: they merely 'venerate' them.


As I told you, I do not place all my trust in a book that has been filtered by the empire of hypocrites. Basically, the Church of Rome has managed to get people to respect and worship its own monuments under different names: the supposed image of Jesus, a copy of the image of Zeus (Jupiter); the supposed image of Saint Michael, a copy of the Roman soldier whom they worshipped as the 'god Mars', wearing a military uniform and armor.


Given that canonization comes from that empire, it is foolish to trust blindly in its absolute faithfulness.


Where Are Jesus' Messages About Idolatry?


When the practice of kneeling before statues, burning incense to them, or lowering one's gaze before them is challenged, the cleric's response is always the same semantic evasion: 'We do not worship them; we only venerate them'. They change the verb, dress up the name, and pretend that the act is no longer idolatry.


However, if we examine the texts under a magnifying glass, a major historical anomaly emerges: the prophets of the Old Testament did not beat around the bush. Isaiah and Habakkuk described the harsh reality of idols in great detail: the cut log, the carved wood, the mute object that cannot see, hear, or walk, and the absolute condemnation of those who bow down before it. If Jesus was mistaken by the crowds for one of the ancient prophets, as Matthew 16 recounts, his discourse should have followed that same direct and unambiguous line.


So why do the Gospels and the epistles of Peter, Paul, and John maintain such a strange silence about the physical description of idolatry? The only direct allusion in the entire New Testament appears in isolation in Revelation 9:20, but it is insufficient given the scale of the problem. Is it logical that the apostles would have omitted such a fundamental warning?


This is where we must stop swallowing the official story whole. Through strategic filtering, the Roman Empire removed all messages that were uncomfortable or too explicit and that affected its interests. It is no coincidence that the Church of Rome recycled the monuments of imperial paganism under new names (the image of Zeus turned into that of Jesus, or the soldier Mars transformed into Saint Michael) to keep the masses kneeling.


Inevitable conclusion: Jesus never founded this religion. It was the Roman Empire that built Christianity upon incomplete texts that had been tampered with and adapted to suit its own purposes.


Proverbs 28:13 He who conceals his sins will not prosper, but whoever confesses and forsakes them will find mercy.


Do you confess that you have sinned through idolatry? I do confess it: I have sinned through idolatry. I knelt before Catholic images and treated them as idols are treated, just as thousands of people do before priests who tell them: 'You do not worship these images; you only worship God under our guidance. But an idolized image is never broken by the person who worships it, and there are people who will call you a 'heretic' if you do.


Do you know when I stopped committing the sin of worshipping images? When I informed myself and read Exodus 20:5, it became clear to me what the sin was, and I stopped doing it. But today, those who know that message look for five legs on a cat to keep demanding that people bend their knees before their images.


God loves those who have dignity and are capable of learning not to bend the knee to anything or anyone.

Daniel 12:10 Many shall be purified, made white, and refined; the wicked shall act wickedly, and none of the wicked shall understand, but the wise shall understand.

Reflections on yin and yang:


From a young age I was indoctrinated into Catholicism, as commonly happens to many young people in Peru, a predominantly Catholic country. I came to believe that its dogmas were the truth and that the Bible, defended by the Church, contained only the truth.


Due to circumstances in life, I came to examine the Bible more deeply. I encountered the message of Exodus 20:5 and immediately stopped being Catholic because I saw in Catholic practices a violation of that commandment regarding the treatment of images: do not bow down to them or honor them.


That revelation led me to believe that the Bible contained other revelations that I still had to discover. Furthermore, because different groups defended that book and its authority was recognized by numerous sectors of the world, I made the mistake of believing the official narrative and assuming that the Bible contained only the truth.


Over time, I was able to read more and found serious contradictions. Then I began to reflect. It is obvious: if Jesus existed and his message was one of justice, it is evident that this message could not please the unjust, because justice contradicts the interests and conduct of those who act unjustly. A just message is embraced by the just, but rejected by the unjust. And if that message had been capable of transforming absolutely all unjust people into just people, everyone would have been transformed and no one would have remained to persecute it. No one would have been an enemy of Jesus or his followers, because everyone would have embraced the original message.


But persecution did take place. If Rome persecuted that message, how can we maintain that the very empire that fought against it ended up truly converting to it? Why believe that what Rome made official is really the original message of Jesus?


No wonder certain teachings never made sense to me, such as turning the other cheek and doing good regardless of whom you are helping, treating everyone equally, regardless of whether they were just or unjust. Those things did not make sense to me, although at that time I was unaware of many of these issues.


Nor did it make sense to me that a just person would give his life for the unjust and forgive them as he died on the cross, when, by all appearances, according to certain messages in the Bible, the murderers did know what they were doing. In the parable of the wicked tenants, they kill the rightful heir to take possession of his property, whereas elsewhere in the Bible the victim appears saying that they should be forgiven because they do not know what they are doing. However, in the parable of the wicked tenants, they do know what they are doing. These contradictions reveal manipulation.


For all these reasons, I have reached the point where I no longer believe in the Bible as a book that contains exclusively the truth. That does not mean that I automatically reject everything it says: I still find some messages that make sense to me, such as the separation between the just and the unjust represented in the parable of the wheat and the tares. But I no longer accept everything it contains as truth simply because an institution or a tradition claims it is so.


My reflection on good and evil: the yin-yang symbol:


Two years before reading Exodus 20:5 for the first time, when I was 20 years old and still ignorant of many things, and was even still Catholic, I had an experience that I would remember many years later.


A fellow student at the IDAT institute, who was also a karate practitioner, drew a picture of yin and yang one day and asked me:


—José, do you know what that means?


I thought about it and finally answered that I did not know.


To help you better understand the story, you should know that he was an atheist, whereas I still believed in Catholicism and was unaware of many of these issues.


I remember that, a few days earlier, after leaving a group assignment, we had passed by a park across from which there was a church. Since I was Catholic, I made the sign of the cross when I saw it. Just imagine! Me, making the sign of the cross. But not only that: I also looked at my classmate and asked him:


—Don't you make the sign of the cross?


Paradoxically, his name was Michael. He answered that he did not, that he was an atheist, that he did not believe in God and that he believed in himself.


When I heard him, I remembered a message from 'A Look at Conscience', by Brother Pablo. It told the testimony of a man who had everything, but who suddenly suffered an accident that he could not control. Then I replied to Michael that such things depended on God, not on oneself. After all, what good is it to believe in oneself if there are things beyond one's control?


Michael remained silent.


Some time later, that same Michael drew the yin-yang symbol and explained its meaning to me. This time, however, I was the one who remained silent, because I did not know what that symbol represented.


Then he told me:


—This means good and evil, that good will always be equal to evil in strength and that both need each other.


Years passed and I remembered that conversation and that symbol again, which I had already seen earlier when I studied kung fu at the age of 14. By then, I had already acquired a great deal of knowledge thanks to my reading and personal experiences. I reflected again on Michael's explanation, with which I had never agreed, since I had never accepted that the struggle between good and evil had to be eternal or that both were equally powerful forces. That was when I reached a different conclusion:


'Well, there always has to be good and evil, the just and the unjust. It is logical: if there is no light, there is no darkness; if there are no just people, there are no unjust people'.


However, the fact that both exist does not mean that they have to be mixed together.


That is why, in my interpretation of the symbol, I removed those dots: the white dot, represented by that white circle inside the black area, and the black dot, that black circle inside the white area. To me, they do not have to be mixed together; they have to be separated.


Furthermore, I believe that good is superior to evil, because evil can imitate good, but never equal it. It is like bronze trying to pass itself off as gold: even if it tries to resemble gold, it does not become gold or acquire the same value.


That is why whatever imitates good, but never manages to equal it, is always inferior, just as gold is superior to any metal that attempts to imitate it.


My interpretation of Daniel 12:10: justice and ignorance:


I disagree with those who say that no one is completely just or unjust. I rather agree with the message of Daniel 12:10, which I interpret as follows: if the just person has some blemish, it does not have to be an unjust side, because that person has no unjust side, but rather ignorance that prevents them from acting fully in accordance with justice and leads them down the wrong path. That is why they need to be guided along the right path through knowledge.


When the just person acquires knowledge and frees themselves from that ignorance, they are purified and, as that verse expresses it, made white. It is like cleaning wheat, removing whatever dirties it.


I maintain that a just person is completely just and achieves perfection when they destroy the ignorance that causes them to stray from the path of justice.


Those who believe that the war between good and evil has to be eternal seem to love violence, because they do not even consider perpetual peace as a possibility. And if they do not consider it, then they have already surrendered to the idea that the conflict will never end.


So, why not make the forces of darkness remain in eternal conflict, but among themselves? If all the carnivorous animals in the wild never stopped fighting among themselves and were incapable of working as a team, peaceful herbivores could live in peace forever.


A certain passage in the Bible evokes for me the idea of an eternal conflict between the condemned and the worms. I do not believe that the condemned love the worms. However, if the worms never die and the condemned do not die either, then they would have an eternal conflict there, though not between good and evil, but between evil and evil.


Isaiah 66:24 says:


'And they shall go out, and look upon the corpses of the men who rebelled against me; for their worm shall never die, nor shall their fire be quenched, and they shall be abhorrent to all mankind'.

39	"The Gospel of Thomas and the Hidden Deception About Pork █


Pointing out contradictions in the New Testament regarding the Law and the Prophets does not make me a defender of the Old Testament. I defend neither testament, nor do I defend either book of the Bible in its entirety against the other. Therefore, I have serious moral objections to many things attributed to the Law of Moses, such as circumcision, as well as to teachings attributed to Jesus, such as the command to love one's enemies. I cannot trust the integrity of texts transmitted by an empire that persecuted and killed people for defending messages that the Roman Empire found intolerable. Temporal coexistence does not imply approval (The Wheat, the Tares, and the Net): Your reasoning regarding the parable of the wheat and the tares or the dragnet in the sea demonstrates that the fact that the wicked and the righteous grow together temporarily—or that the sun rises on both—does not mean that God loves the wicked nor that they share the same destiny. The King and the Condemned


A king had a great prison.


Within it were murderers, thieves, traitors, and other criminals. There were also some innocent men who remained imprisoned until the day of their trials would arrive.


Every morning the king ordered that everyone be given water.


Every evening he ordered that everyone be given food.


Seeing this, many of the kingdom's inhabitants would say:


—Our king loves all the prisoners.


In those days, Gabriel, having emerged victorious from a conflict against certain gods who had rebelled against the God of gods and who, after committing injustice of their own free will, conspired to make the masses believe that the God of gods granted forgiveness even to those who had not made themselves worthy of it, came to that people.

Gabriel asked them:


—Why do you say that?


The inhabitants replied:


—Because he gives them water.


—Because he gives them food.


—Because he allows them to remain alive.


Then Gabriel asked:


—And when will the judgment take place?


—In a few months' time, they answered.


The months passed.


The day of judgment arrived.


The innocent were set free.

Then Gabriel turned again to the inhabitants and asked:

No one answered.

The inhabitants remained silent.

https://gabriels.work/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/idi02-abandoning-satan-and-his-angels-in-hell.gif


https://gabriels.work/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/la-victoria-de-las-fuerzas-de-la-coherencia.gif


My own comments:


If you have ever read Matthew 5, you have probably noticed that it teaches that we should love our enemies in order to imitate the perfection of God, who makes His sun rise on the good and the evil and sends rain on the just and the unjust.


But this raises a question: does the fact that the sun shines on someone really mean that God loves that person?


The same reasoning could be applied to Sodom. The sun rose on Lot and on the people of Sodom alike. Yet when judgment came, Lot was preserved and the others were destroyed.


Likewise, in the parable of the wheat and the weeds, both grow together under the same sun. Yet in the end, the wheat is gathered and the weeds are burned.


The same is true of the good and bad fish in the net. They remain together for a time, but they do not share the same destiny.


And the same is true in the parable of the king and the condemned. The king gave food and water both to the innocent and to the criminals. Yet when judgment came, he set some free and condemned the others.


Therefore, sharing temporary benefits does not necessarily mean sharing the same favor or the same final destiny.


If God loved everyone without exception, why do the prophecies speak of the elect? Why do they speak of a separation between the righteous and the wicked? Why are some preserved while others are destroyed?


Many are called, but few are chosen.


If all were loved in exactly the same way and for exactly the same purpose, then all would be chosen.


But if not all are chosen, then the claim that God loves everyone equally does not fit easily with the recurring biblical images of judgment, separation, and election.


Some will say that I am rejecting the words of Jesus.


No.


What I reject is the idea that Jesus could have contradicted himself on a matter so fundamental.


I cannot accept that the same man who spoke of the wheat and the weeds, the good fish and the bad fish, the chosen and the rejected, the final separation of the righteous and the wicked, was also teaching that God's perfection consists in loving everyone indiscriminately.


One voice makes distinctions. The other erases them.


One speaks of judgment. The other appears to dissolve it.


One separates the wheat from the weeds. The other asks us to believe that the farmer feels exactly the same about both.


I do not believe the problem lies with God.


I do not believe the problem lies with Jesus.


I believe the problem lies with those who later held the message in their hands.


And I find it more than a coincidence that some of the doctrines most favorable to the persecutors are precisely the ones that seem to stand in tension with the rest of the message.


When a king gives one command, and later another contradictory command is attributed to that same king, I do not conclude that the king contradicted himself.


I conclude that someone spoke in the king's name.


And when I find two incompatible messages attributed to the same source, I do not assume that the source contradicted itself.


I assume that someone altered the message.


Think about it: the very empire that persecuted the followers of the message eventually controlled the councils, preserved the texts, shaped the doctrine, and taught that the righteous man died for his enemies, forgave his executioners, and loved even those who condemned him.


I find that far too convenient to ignore.


I do not need to know the name of the forger to suspect a forgery.


It is enough to compare the messages and see that they do not agree.


So each person must draw his own conclusions.


But as for me, I would rather suspect men than suspect the consistency of God.


And if you think I am merely building an argument from parables, consider the language that the Scriptures themselves use when describing God's feelings.


Read Nahum 1.


There you will find a jealous God, an avenging God, a God who does not acquit His enemies, a God who reserves wrath for His adversaries, yet also a God who knows those who trust in Him.


That is not the portrait of a God who loves everyone without distinction. It is the portrait of a God who distinguishes between friend and enemy, between those who trust in Him and those who oppose Him.


The same pattern appears in Exodus 20. God describes Himself as a jealous God, one who visits iniquity upon those who hate Him, yet shows steadfast love to those who love Him and keep His commandments.


Once again, love is not presented as universal and indiscriminate. It is directed toward those who love Him, while judgment is reserved for those who oppose Him.


This is precisely why I struggle to reconcile these passages with the claim that God's perfection consists in loving everyone equally. The prophets, the law, the parables of judgment, the wheat and the weeds, the good and bad fish, the chosen and the rejected, all seem to point in a different direction.


Either these messages belong together, or they do not.


And if they do not, then the real question is not whether a contradiction exists. The real question is: who introduced it?

How Isaiah 28, Psalm 2, Psalm 118, and Daniel 12 Connect in the Prophecy Concerning the Great Deception


Isaiah's prophecy concerning the refuge of lies cannot be considered completely fulfilled while deception remains and the promised foundation in Zion has not been established in its ultimate sense. Isaiah 28:15 exposes the refuge of lies, while Isaiah 28:16 declares that Jehovah lays a precious cornerstone in Zion, a firm foundation.


Psalm 2:6 declares:


'Yet have I set my king upon my holy hill of Zion.'


Zion represents Jehovah's chosen people under the rule of His appointed King. In this prophetic interpretation, the establishment of the King upon Zion is connected with the ultimate vindication of the righteous.


Psalm 118 reinforces this connection. The psalm begins by declaring the goodness of Jehovah and calling upon Israel to proclaim it:


Psalm 118:1-2: 'O give thanks unto Jehovah; for he is good: because his mercy endureth for ever. Let Israel now say, that his mercy endureth for ever.'


The psalm then speaks of the gates of righteousness through which the righteous enter:


Psalm 118:19-20: 'Open to me the gates of righteousness: I will go into them, and I will praise Jehovah: This gate of Jehovah, into which the righteous shall enter.'


The same psalm identifies the stone rejected by the builders as having become the headstone of the corner:


Psalm 118:22: 'The stone which the builders refused is become the head stone of the corner.'


These passages establish a meaningful connection between the stone, the gates of righteousness, and the people who enter through them. The psalm begins by calling upon Israel to proclaim the goodness of Jehovah and then identifies the righteous as those who enter Jehovah's gate. In this prophetic interpretation, the true Israel of God is constituted by the righteous, rather than being defined merely by ancestry or political identity.


The stone rejected by the builders in Psalm 118:22 connects with the precious cornerstone established in Zion in Isaiah 28:16 and with the King established upon Zion in Psalm 2:6. Together, these passages point to the establishment of Jehovah's rule and the vindication of the righteous.


Daniel 12:1 completes this prophetic connection by describing Michael rising up at the time of the end to defend God's people during a time of unprecedented distress. The people whom Michael defends are the righteous, who constitute the true Israel of God. Their ultimate vindication is connected with the establishment of Jehovah's rule and the fulfillment of the prophecies concerning Zion.

Religious Distribution in Chile (1992 Census vs. Today)

1992:
They represented approximately 76.7% of the population. The Catholic Church maintained enormous social, institutional, and moral influence, further supported by the role played by the Vicariate of Solidarity during the military regime in defending human rights.

1992:
They barely reached 5.8%. Declaring oneself to have no religion was very uncommon and was even socially penalized in certain circles.


Today:
It is the fastest-growing group in the country, exceeding 37%–40% in recent surveys, especially concentrated among young people and in urban areas.


Key factors behind the change over these 30 years


Emergence of church abuse scandals:
The widespread allegations and exposure of sexual and institutional abuse within the Chilean Catholic Church (with prominent cases emerging from the 2010s onward) caused a collapse in public trust and accelerated mass disaffiliation.


Rapid secularization of society:
As Chile experienced accelerated economic growth and greater sociocultural openness during the 1990s and 2000s, lifestyles and legislation became more secular (for example, with the approval of civil divorce in 2004, abortion on three grounds in 2017, and same-sex marriage in 2021).


Generational change:
Generations born from the 1990s onward grew up in an environment with greater access to information and cultural diversity, moving away from the traditional religious structures in which their parents and grandparents had been raised.


José:


'Can you bring up which those cases are? If possible, include links to news articles or other sources that document them.'


Gemini:


The cases that exposed this crisis in Chile combined abuses of power, sexual abuse, and persistent institutional cover-up by the senior hierarchy.


The Karadima Case (The Turning Point)


Fernando Karadima was parish priest of the Church of the Sacred Heart of Jesus of El Bosque (Santiago), a parish of the Chilean elite. For decades, he acted as an influential spiritual leader with the tacit support or concealment of high-ranking prelates.


The exposure: In 2010, his victims —such as James Hamilton, Juan Carlos Cruz, and José Andrés Murillo— made public the abuses of power, psychological abuse, and sexual abuse they had suffered when they were young.


Impact:
In 2011, the Vatican declared him guilty of sexual abuse. In 2018, Pope Francis permanently dismissed him from the clerical state.


Judicial consequence: In 2019, the Chilean justice system determined that the Archdiocese of Santiago had engaged in negligence and concealment, ordering the institution to compensate the victims.

2.
The 'Cura Tato' Case (José Andrés Aguirre)


One of the first media cases to alert public opinion in the early 2000s.


The incident: Aguirre was investigated and tried for ongoing sexual abuse against minors.


Conviction: In 2004, he was sentenced by the Chilean civil justice system to 12 years in prison for sexual abuse offenses against minors. He was one of the first priests to be criminally convicted in the country.


3.
The 2018 Papal Visit and the Bishops' Crisis


The decisive moment in the collapse of ecclesial credibility occurred in January 2018 during Pope Francis's visit to Chile.


The conflict: The Pope publicly defended the Bishop of Osorno, Juan Barros (accused by victims of having witnessed and covered up Karadima's abuses), describing the accusations as 'slander'.


The reaction: Public outrage in Chile was massive. Following this, the Pope sent the Vatican investigator Charles Scicluna to investigate on the ground.


The mass resignation: After receiving the report on systematic cover-up within the Chilean Church, Pope Francis summoned all the Chilean bishops to Rome in May 2018. Following the meeting, the entire Chilean episcopate (34 bishops) submitted their resignations en masse, an unprecedented event in the history of the Catholic Church.


4.
The Prosecutor's Office's Mass Investigations (2018–Present)


Beginning in 2018, Chile's Public Prosecutor's Office opened investigations ex officio and seized documentation from the bishoprics of several regions.


The prosecutors went on to investigate more than 200 members of the clergy (including bishops and priests from dioceses such as Rancagua, Valparaíso, and Santiago) over hundreds of allegations of abuse and cover-up.

José:


These revelations occurred all over the world. What catches my attention is how, only in Chile, so many people woke up that it became very noticeable; in the rest of the countries...


Gemini:


The phenomenon in Chile is unique in the region. Although scandals involving abuse and ecclesiastical cover-up have occurred globally (such as in Ireland, the United States, Australia, and Germany), the social response in Chile led to one of the fastest declines in religious affiliation and religious institutionalism in the world.


There are several social, institutional, and cultural factors that explain why in Chile this 'awakening' or disengagement was so forceful compared with other Latin American countries:


1.
The Breakdown of Historical 'Moral Capital'


In Chile, the Catholic Church held an exceptionally high ethical standing that it did not have in other countries. During the military dictatorship of Augusto Pinochet, the Church — through the Vicariate of Solidarity — was the main protective shield for victims of human rights violations.


When it was revealed that the same institution that had defended people against State violence was concealing abuse against young people and children in its own parishes, the collapse of trust was not gradual, but a direct institutional trauma. The clash between the country's image of moral authority and reality accelerated collective disillusionment.


2.
The Elitization of the Conflict (Karadima Case)


Unlike other countries where cases occurred in rural or peripheral parishes with little access to the media, the Karadima Case took place in the parish of the Sacred Heart of El Bosque (Providencia), the heart of Santiago's economic, political, and social elite.


The victims belonged to influential and professional families (doctors, journalists, philosophers) with direct access to the media, legal resources, and networks to sustain a media and judicial battle for years without being silenced. This prevented the ecclesiastical hierarchy from being able to 'put out' the issue in the media.


3.
The Strategic Error of the Papal Visit (2018)


In most countries, the figure of the Pope tends to function as a buffer for local crises. In Chile, the opposite occurred: Francis's visit in January 2018 acted as the final trigger.


By publicly labeling the accusations against Bishop Juan Barros as 'slander', the Pope generated widespread indignation among citizens. When the Vatican had to correct course by sending Charles Scicluna and demanding the resignation of all the bishops, the Chilean hierarchy was left completely discredited before its own community.


4.
A Society Undergoing an Accelerated Process of Secularization


Chile had already been undergoing profound sociocultural transformations since the 1990s:


Accelerated economic growth and expanded access to higher education.
Mass access to the internet and social networks in a predominantly urban population.
Legislative changes that challenged traditional dogma (divorce, abortion under three specific grounds, same-sex marriage).


The abuse crisis did not create secularization by itself, but it functioned as the final catalyst for millions of people who already had doubts or cultural distance to definitively sever their ties with the institution.


The Comparison with the Rest of the Region


In the rest of Latin America: In countries such as Mexico, Colombia, or Peru, the Catholic religion remains deeply linked to cultural identity, local festivities, and community structures, generating greater resistance to mass disengagement despite existing allegations.


The global pattern: Chile's behavior is much more similar to that of Ireland or Spain, where systematic revelations led to an accelerated emptying of churches over the course of one or two decades, leaving the institution with historically low levels of trust.


José:

Gemini:


The case of Chile demonstrates precisely that: when documented information, facts, and evidence come to light publicly without censorship, the institutional and social impact is inevitable.


The combination of victims willing to speak, rigorous investigative journalism, and mass access to information broke decades of concealment and silence. It shows that societies are not monolithic and do not remain blind forever; when the contradictions and abuses of structures of power are exposed, people reassess their convictions and make informed decisions about what or whom to trust.

26	"The images of the Devil were passed off as images of the saints█


From Mars to Saint Michael: How the Roman Empire Gave Its Statues Wings: the image of the Devil was passed off as the image of the saint.


[BLOCK 1 - THE ORIGIN OF ICONOGRAPHY AND THE PROHIBITION]


We have been told over and over again that this image represents good and evil: on the right, Jesus; on the left, the devil. We were taught that evil is the dark-skinned man with short hair, while good is the white-skinned man with long hair and a halo or solar crown over his head.


https://gabriels.work/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/ary_scheffer_-_the_temptation_of_christ_1854-1-1.jpg


But before taking this for granted, ask yourself a key question: where does this version of events actually come from?


It comes from the Roman Empire and from the ecclesiastical structure that inherited its power. The same empire that executed Jesus openly worshiped solar gods.


This is where the logic breaks down. There are ancient texts such as Exodus 20 and Deuteronomy 4 that establish a clear and categorical prohibition: do not worship or bow down before any representation, whether of the heavens, the earth, men, the sun, or the moon.


If the Roman Empire worshiped the sun, persecuted the original message, and then built the official Church… why should we assume that its conversion was sincere? If today people are required to bow before representations and elements that directly replicate solar symbolism, are we not seeing exactly the same imperial worship, but under a different name?


The text of Habakkuk 2 puts it with devastating clarity: What good is a mute image? Can it possibly teach anything?


A sculpture or a painting does not hear, does not understand, and has no spirit. The prohibition against worshiping them is not a blind dogma; it is a rational position. An image cannot do anything for you.


This does not mean accepting as valid everything that has survived to the present day. If these texts passed for centuries through the filter and editing of imperial hands, textual manipulation is not merely a possibility: it is a historical certainty. They rewrote the accounts of their time and retouched the oldest writings to consolidate their power.


[BLOCK 2 - THE CONTRADICTION OF LONG HAIR]


Let us clarify something fundamental: this is not about defending the biblical text or assuming that everything it contains is authentic. History shows that the manuscript that reached us passed through filters and interpolations to align with the interests of imperial power. However, even within these texts, their own logic exposes the cracks in the visual tradition that was sold to us.


Let us take the representation of long hair as an example. Official iconography portrayed a long-haired Jesus, but if we analyze letters such as 1 Corinthians 11, the narrative collapses through simple internal consistency.


In that writing, Paul explicitly states two things: first, that he is an imitator of Christ; and second, that for a man it is a dishonor or a shame to let his hair grow long.


The mathematical conclusion is unavoidable: neither that apostle nor the historical Jesus wore long hair. It is a matter of basic logical honesty. No one who claims to faithfully follow a teacher would call the very appearance of that teacher a 'shame.'


Where, then, does the image of the man with long hair, light skin, and a solar crown come from? It does not come from historical reality or from the context of the first century. It comes from Roman aesthetic assimilation, which took the features of its own pagan deities and gave them the name of Jesus to facilitate religious control. We were sold a portrait fabricated by the same empire that destroyed the original message.


[BLOCK 3 - THE FALLACY IN THE DESERT TEMPTATION]


We have also been told that this painting represents the supposed temptation of Jesus in the desert. However, when we analyze the texts that the official structure used to support that account —such as Matthew 4—, the seams once again come apart.


In that passage, Psalm 91 is quoted to support the scene: 'He shall give his angels charge over thee, to keep thee in all thy ways. They shall bear thee up in their hands, lest thou dash thy foot against a stone.' But if you read Psalm 91 in its entirety, the protagonist of that text is someone to whom nothing happens, someone at whose side a thousand and ten thousand fall on his right, but harm does not reach him.


That scenario was never fulfilled in the historical Jesus. He was executed; he fell before imperial violence, and many did not fall before him.


This painting was presented to us as the pious account of an overcome temptation, when in reality they passed off a text about protection and invulnerability that was not fulfilled, forcing it to give validity to the narrative they constructed. It is another example of how the empire took incomplete fragments to force prophecies and consolidate a story tailored to its interests.


[BLOCK 4 - THE CONTRADICTION OF THE KINGDOMS AND WORSHIP]


The temptation account adds another element that collapses through simple deductive analysis. We are told that Satan offered Jesus all the kingdoms of the earth in exchange for bowing down and worshiping him.


Now, think about it coldly: if Satan rules all the kingdoms of the earth, is it logical to assume that the books and official canons approved by the empires of this world contain an intact truth? Or is it not more logical to conclude that those same official writings are infiltrated and edited by those who control those kingdoms?


But the contradiction becomes even more evident when analyzing the behavior attributed to Jesus in other passages. In the account of the ten lepers who were healed, the narrative shows Jesus allowing the only one who returned to bow at his feet, stating that only he gave glory to God.


Here we must ask the fundamental questions: Would a righteous man who respects God ask other human beings to kneel before him? Would he demand exactly the same thing that Satan supposedly asked him to do in the desert?


We are presented with a character molded in the image of imperial kings and solar gods, tolerating and encouraging people to bow before him. And to make matters worse, in passages such as Hebrews 1:6 they claim that all the angels must worship him.


This clashes head-on with older writings such as Psalm 97, where it is clearly established that worship belongs exclusively to Jehovah —the Creator who is not a creature and who cannot die—. Jesus, on the other hand, according to the account itself, died.


The pieces fabricated by the empire simply do not fit.


https://gabriels.work/wp-content/uploads/2026/08/idi02-this-is-the-sun-god-of-the-roma-empire-text.jpg


https://gabriels.work/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/jose-galindo-2008-33-anos.jpg


[BLOCK 5 - THE MANIPULATION IN THE QUOTATION OF PSALM 69]


To dispel any doubt about how these writings were manipulated and assembled, let us analyze a concrete example that was sold to us as the supreme act of love, but which completely falls apart when the original sources are examined.


The traditional narrative tells us that, while on the cross, Jesus was given vinegar to drink in order to 'fulfill a scripture,' and that at that very moment he prayed for his executioners, saying: 'Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do.' We were taught to see this as an example of forgiveness carried through to the final breath. But what does the cited prophecy actually say?


The reference to vinegar comes from Psalm 69. However, when you read the full context of that psalm, the tone is not one of forgiveness or love toward enemies; it is the exact opposite.


The protagonist of that psalm is not asking forgiveness for those who are torturing him. He is asking for judgment: he demands that the banquet of his enemies become a trap, that their eyes be blinded, and that wrath fall upon them.


How is it possible for an act presented as supreme forgiveness to cite as its support a text in which the protagonist cries out for the ruin of his aggressors?


Here the seam of Roman editing is exposed. They took isolated verses to force the fulfillment of supposed prophecies, creating a pious narrative that promoted resignation and submission before the oppressor, while deliberately ignoring the original context of the writing. We were sold a story made over by the same hands that edited the book.


[BLOCK 6 - THE INVERSION OF THE IMAGE]


In light of this evidence and these historical contradictions, the conclusion is unavoidable: the Roman Empire never adopted or preached the original teaching it persecuted; rather, it absorbed it and molded it to suit its own purposes.


That is why the definitive question must be asked by looking directly at the iconography that was imposed upon us.


Look carefully at this representation. If the historical framework of first-century Judea indicates that the local population had darker complexions and wore short hair —in accordance with the letters attributed to Paul—, it is contradictory that the figure of the 'righteous man' was drawn with Nordic features, long hair, and imperial solar elements.


Could it be that history was completely inverted? Who really represents the oppressor in that painting, and who represents the righteous man who refused to submit to the empire?


https://gabriels.work/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/idi02-me-in-my-youth-2.jpg


The empire dressed its own image in the mantle of holiness and portrayed the features of the subjected people under the figure of the adversary. We were taught to venerate the aesthetic of the dominator and reject the actual appearance of the persecuted.


Think about it. The answer is not found in blind faith, but in questioning whom the image before which you kneel benefits.

The Lazarus Paradox: Does Man Die Only Once or Twice?


Does Lazarus walk among us... and is he over 2,000 years old? If Jesus resurrected Lazarus, the question is simple: Did he die again... or would he be nearly 2,000 years old today? Hebrews 9:27 clearly states: 'Man dies only once.' But in Juan 11:43-44 it is claimed: 'Lazarus, come out! And he who had died came out.' Then only three options remain:


Option 1: Lazarus did not die again. If so, he would be nearly 2,000 years old. Has anyone seen him?


Option 2: Lazarus did die again. Then man does not die 'only once.'


Option 3: The story was added centuries later and we were told something that never happened. In short: someone invented it... and millions never questioned it.


Three options. Think carefully: Which of them is the most logical? A Roman emperor could have thought: 'I will say that everyone should worship him (Hebrews 1:6), I will associate him with Zeus, I will attribute miracles to him, and in the end everyone will worship Jupiter, our Roman god.'


The Bible also speaks of other resurrections: 'Little girl, get up!' (Mark 5:41) — and Jairus' daughter lived again. 'Young man, I say to you, get up.' (Luke 7:14) — and the son of the widow of Nain got up. Matthew 27:52-53 claims that after the death of Jesus: 'The tombs were opened, and many bodies of the saints who had fallen asleep were raised...' The Old Testament also recounts resurrections: the son of the widow of Zarephath (1 Kings 17:17-24), the son of the Shunammite (2 Kings 4:32-37), and a dead man who comes back to life upon touching Elisha's bones (2 Kings 13:20-21).


The only logical option is: Option 3: The stories were added later and never occurred. Because this situation is absurd: The press interviews the resurrected Lazarus, and he tells them: 'Hebrews 9:27 says that man dies only once, that's why I'm still here.'

13	"Has Isaiah's Prophecy About the Desolate Cities Already Been Fulfilled? █


According to the Bible, Isaiah says that some people will not see:


Isaiah 6:10:


'Make the heart of this people dull, and make their ears heavy, and blind their eyes, lest they see with their eyes, and hear with their ears, and understand with their heart, and be converted, and be healed.'


Isaiah 6:11-12:


Then I said, 'How long, Lord?' And He answered, 'Until the cities lie waste without inhabitant, and the houses are without people, and the land becomes utterly desolate; until the LORD has removed men far away, and the abandoned places are multiplied in the midst of the land.'


And regarding other people, Isaiah says that they must see:


Isaiah 42:6-7:


'I, the LORD, have called you in righteousness, and will hold your hand; I will keep you and give you as a covenant to the people, as a light to the nations, to open the eyes of the blind, to bring the prisoners out of the prison, and those who dwell in darkness out of the prison house.'


The Bible also says that Jesus came to bring judgment:


John 9:39:


'And Jesus said, 'For judgment I have come into this world, so that those who do not see may see, and those who see may become blind.''


The contradiction presented:


If Jesus had come to fulfill the prophecies of the final judgment, then the final judgment would already have brought justice through His judgment, yet we see that justice does not prevail. Therefore, that prophecy was not fulfilled through Him, because for it to be fulfilled in someone, that person must see justice triumph:


Isaiah 42:1-4:


'Behold My servant, whom I uphold; My chosen one, in whom My soul delights. I have put My Spirit upon Him; He will bring justice to the nations.


He will not cry out, nor raise His voice, nor cause it to be heard in the streets.


A bruised reed He will not break, and a smoldering wick He will not extinguish; He will faithfully bring forth justice.


He will not fail nor be discouraged until He has established justice on the earth; and the coastlands shall wait for His law.'


However, the Bible presents Jesus as fulfilling that prophecy:


Matthew 12:16-21:


'And He strictly warned them not to make Him known, so that what was spoken through the prophet Isaiah might be fulfilled:


'Behold My Servant, whom I have chosen; My Beloved, in whom My soul is well pleased. I will put My Spirit upon Him, and He shall proclaim judgment to the Gentiles.


He will not quarrel, nor cry out, nor will anyone hear His voice in the streets.


A bruised reed He will not break, and a smoldering wick He will not extinguish, until He leads judgment to victory.


And in His name the Gentiles will hope.''


Here another inconsistency arises, not only because justice does not reign on the earth, but also because the Bible repeatedly shows Jesus speaking loudly and raising His voice before crowds, as in the Sermon on the Mount:


Matthew 5:1-2:


'And seeing the multitudes, He went up on a mountain; and after He sat down, His disciples came to Him. Then He opened His mouth and taught them, saying...'


Consider this paradox:


Matthew 5:6:


'Blessed are those who hunger and thirst for justice, for they shall be satisfied.'


In Matthew 5:6 He blesses those who hunger and thirst for justice—that is, the righteous. But further down we read:


Matthew 5:38-39:


'You have heard that it was said, 'An eye for an eye and a tooth for a tooth.' But I tell you, do not resist an evil person. Instead, if anyone slaps you on the right cheek, turn to him the other also.'


Is hungering and thirsting for justice the same as wanting to receive more attacks from an evil aggressor?


Matthew 5:17 says that He came to fulfill the Law, yet the Law commands that 'eye for eye,' which the very same passage alters:


Matthew 5:17:


'Do not think that I have come to abolish the Law or the Prophets; I have not come to abolish them but to fulfill them.'


Matthew 5 portrays Him as fulfilling the Law that commands the removal of evil, while at the same time portraying Him as instructing people not to resist evil:


Deuteronomy 19:19-21:


'Then you shall do to him as he intended to do to his brother; so you shall remove the evil from among you. And those who remain shall hear and fear, and shall never again commit any such evil among you. Your eye shall not pity: life for life, eye for eye, tooth for tooth, hand for hand, foot for foot.'


It can be concluded that the Roman institutional structure did not preserve the original message intact, but instead intervened in the texts. That alteration is not limited only to the words attributed to Jesus, but also extends to the traditions of the scribes and to the prophets of the Old Testament.


This is not about placing the Old Testament above the New Testament, but about pointing out the evident fractures throughout the entire body of the book.


A brief piece of evidence of these internal contradictions:


On the one hand, the covenant's command requires performing ritual cuts on one's own body:


Genesis 17:10–11:


'This is My covenant, which you shall keep, between Me and you and your descendants after you: Every male among you shall be circumcised. You shall circumcise the flesh of your foreskin, and it shall be the sign of the covenant between Me and you.'


On the other hand, the same law explicitly forbids making any kind of cut or mark on the skin:


Leviticus 19:28:


'You shall not make any cuts in your flesh for the dead, nor tattoo any marks on yourselves: I am the LORD.'


If the text commands, in one book, the performance of a mandatory bodily cut as a sign of belonging, but in another forbids interventions or cuts in the flesh, the lack of internal cohesion within the editorial collection is exposed.


If Jesus did not fulfill all the prophecies attributed to Him, when will they be fulfilled? When will Isaiah 6 be fulfilled?


Do you believe that by not resisting the one who is evil they overcome the dragon?


Revelation 12:17:


'Then the dragon became furious with the woman and went off to wage war against the rest of her offspring, those who keep the commandments of God and hold to the testimony of Jesus Christ.'


This is the traditional answer usually given to this contradiction:


'Official theology argues that the prophecy of Isaiah 6 has a 'dual application' or a two-stage fulfillment. According to this interpretation, the prophecy was first partially fulfilled during the Babylonian exile, and then had a second fulfillment in the days of Jesus to explain why most of the people did not accept Him (using the blindness of Isaiah 6:10 as a divine decree). Thus, they claim that the text does not contradict itself, but rather unfolds progressively.'


Now look at how we dismiss that traditional position:


To uphold that 'dual application,' institutional exegesis commits a blatant act of manipulation: it cuts the text wherever it is convenient.


They use the first part of the prophecy to justify the blindness and excuse why there was no visible triumph of justice, but they deliberately ignore the conclusion of the same passage in verse 13:


Isaiah 6:13:


'But yet a tenth shall remain in it, and it shall again be consumed; yet as a terebinth tree or as an oak, whose stump remains when it is cut down, so the holy seed shall be its stump.'


If Isaiah 6 had already been fulfilled in the past under that traditional framework:


Where is that 'holy seed' (zera kodesh)? Where is the real restoration of the stump that brings true justice to the earth?


A prophecy cannot be declared 'fulfilled' by taking only the judgment and the blindness while ignoring the requirement of the reemergence of the holy seed and the establishment of the justice promised in Isaiah 42.


If we isolate Isaiah 6:11–12 to claim that it 'was already fulfilled' merely through physical destruction or exile, then verse 13 is left hanging and the prophecy remains incomplete.


The exact conclusion of the chapter raises the critical point that dismantles the idea of a past or final fulfillment:


Isaiah 6:13:


'But yet a tenth shall remain in it, and it shall again be consumed; yet as a terebinth tree or as an oak, whose stump remains when it is cut down, so the holy seed shall be its stump.'


Here lies the heart of the matter:


A cycle that does not end with desolation: The text does not say that everything ends after the devastation; it says that whatever remains will again be consumed until only a 'stump' remains.


The identity of the 'holy seed': If the blindness and devastation were the judgment upon the corrupt masses or the hardened people, then the 'holy seed' (zera kodesh) represents the genuine remnant, the pure root that does not succumb to corruption or to the imposed blindness.


If it is maintained that Isaiah 6 was already fully fulfilled in the past (whether in Babylon or during the Roman era):


Where was the real restoration of the stump? The later institutions (including the imperial structure that compiled and edited the texts) kept the people under spiritual blindness and subjugation, without the manifestation of the triumphant justice spoken of by Isaiah.


The dilemma of the seed: If the 'holy seed' were the later institutionalized religious structure, then the persistence of the moral and textual contradictions within the canonized writings would make no sense.


Therefore, when the structure of the passage is analyzed, the prophecy of Isaiah 6 cannot be regarded as an event of the past or as something that an institutional system has already brought to completion. The 'holy seed' points to people capable of being set free from a system that wants them on their knees. This fits with the prophecies in Daniel 12:1–7, where a holy people and freedom for that people are spoken of.

The use of Isaiah against the Law and the dietary contradiction: In Matthew 15:1–11, the Gospels quote Jesus using Isaiah to rebuke those who insisted on keeping the traditional law, concluding with the statement that 'it is not what enters the mouth that defiles a man.' However, the prophet Isaiah himself directly contradicts this idea: in Isaiah 65:3–5 and Isaiah 66:17, the prophet explicitly condemns those who eat pork and abominable things, making it clear that, according to the prophetic vision, even in the time of final judgment there are foods that do defile a human being.


The invention of the doctrine of the virgin birth (Isaiah 7:14–16): Both Christianity and Islam embraced the doctrine that Gabriel announced a virgin birth in fulfillment of Isaiah (Matthew 1 / Qur'an 19). But when the original text of Isaiah 7 is examined, it becomes clear that it does not announce Jesus, nor does it speak of a 'perpetual virgin':


The sign was given specifically to King Ahaz and was to be fulfilled immediately, before the child knew how to distinguish between good and evil.


Isaiah speaks of a young woman (almáh), not of a woman who would remain a virgin after giving birth.


The historical fulfillment occurred with Hezekiah, the faithful king in the days of Ahaz, who destroyed the bronze serpent (2 Kings 18:4–7), had God with him (Immanuel), and witnessed the defeat of Assyria prophesied by Isaiah (2 Kings 19:35–37).


The accommodative use of applying fragments out of context demonstrates that the original meaning of the text was not respected, but rather re-edited and forced in order to indoctrinate.

The Roman Distortion of Hosea 6:2 and the Chronological Mismatch


Apocalyptic prophecies and their dogmatic interpretation not only incur identity contradictions, but also break the chronology in order to conceal the true restoration of the righteous people.


We can observe this here:


Revelation 12:7–10:


'Then there was a great war in heaven: Michael and his angels fought against the dragon... And the great dragon was cast out... he was cast to the earth, and his angels were cast out with him. Therefore rejoice, O heavens, and you who dwell in them.'


Revelation 12:12, 17:


'Woe to the inhabitants of the earth and of the sea! For the devil has come down to you with great wrath... Then the dragon was enraged with the woman, and he went to make war with the rest of her offspring, those who keep the commandments of God and have the testimony of Jesus Christ.'


The book of Revelation fragments the scene by presenting the 'inhabitants of heaven' as the victors on one hand, but then shows the righteous within the camp of the 'inhabitants of the earth and the sea' once again suffering the attack of the dragon who was supposedly already defeated once and for all, as though the righteous had not truly triumphed.


If the narrative itself states that the victors despised their lives unto death in order to overcome the dragon, this directly evokes those who preferred death rather than eating pork—such as the testimony of 2 Maccabees 7, where physical surrender took place under the certainty of inheriting eternal life.


Would they then have to return to life in order to be attacked and die once again? Would they have to return to life in order to accept a dogma created by other peoples foreign to their own, in which they are commanded to worship a man under the absurdity that he is man and at the same time God, and whom imperial tradition furthermore portrays with the same visual appearance as Zeus—the very pagan god worshiped by those who executed them? Would they rise again only to hear the aberration that eating pork no longer defiles (Matthew 15:11, 1 Corinthians 10:27), or that they must bow before a created being (Hebrews 1:6)? Would they truly have to be attacked and die once more? If they had to die again, that new existence would never be eternal life.


To be in heaven is to be with God: 'To be in heaven' or with God does not refer to an incorporeal physical place among the clouds, but rather to the condition of being under His protection and of God being with someone. As Psalm 118:6–7 states: 'The LORD is with me; I will not fear. What can man do to me?'


The dead do not wage war. It is the living righteous who are with God, and being with Him does not initially exempt them from facing war against the hostile on the earth.


In order to divert this chronological line and conceal the awakening of this side, Roman theology took the prophecy of Hosea 6:2 and falsely applied it to the resurrection of Jesus after 48 hours:


Hosea 6:1–2:


'Come, and let us return to the LORD; for He has torn us, and He will heal us; He has struck us, and He will bind us up. After two days He will give us life; on the third day He will raise us up, and we shall live before Him.'


The prophecy does not describe Jesus returning to life after a literal 48 hours, but rather the chronological scale of a collective people who reincarnate.


Under the prophetic equivalence in which one day represents one thousand years (Psalm 90:4):


After two days (after 48 hours / 2,000 years): Corresponds to the period of dispersion and ignorance that has elapsed since the beginning of this era.


The third day (the third millennium): Is the present period in which we now live, when that righteous side returns to biological life.


One of the pillars of Roman dogma is that salvation is attained solely by believing that Jesus rose from the dead. However, when this premise is compared with the prophetic texts that the Church used to support it, the figure of a resurrected and perfect Jesus collapses completely:


The heir who sins and is punished (Psalm 118): In the parable of the wicked tenants (Matthew 21:33–44), the Church attributes to Jesus the rejected stone of Psalm 118:22. Nevertheless, Psalm 118 describes a righteous man who sins, is punished by God ('The LORD has chastened me severely, but He has not given me over to death'), and then passes through the gate of righteousness. If this referred to the resurrected Jesus descending from heaven, he could not sin, since he would already know the whole truth. By contrast, if it refers to the righteous seed who reincarnates, its initial passage through ignorance explains why it sins, is rebuked, and is later purified. If he does not return from the clouds, he himself is among that holy seed, but has no way of knowing it, because a new body retains no memories experienced in another body that has already been destroyed.


The confession of sin and the betrayal (Psalm 41): Theology claims that Jesus never sinned and knew from the beginning who would betray him. Yet Psalm 41:4, 9—forced by dogma into being a prophecy about Jesus—speaks of a righteous man who admits having sinned ('LORD, have mercy on me; heal my soul, for I have sinned against You') and who naively trusted the one who betrayed him ('Even my own familiar friend, in whom I trusted, who ate my bread, has lifted up his heel against me'). Trusting a traitor never fits the narrative of an omniscient Jesus, but it does fit a righteous man reincarnated on earth.


Multiple betrayers and the myth of Judas (Psalm 109): Psalm 41 does not speak of a single betrayer, but of several enemies. Once this passage collapses as a prophecy fulfilled in Jesus, the deception that Psalm 109 was fulfilled also collapses: a fabricated dogma that invented the figure of a single traitor (Judas). The falsehood of this dogma is exposed within the contradictory accounts of the Romanized text itself—contradictions deliberately created as a strategic device to reinforce the central falsehood while diverting attention toward secondary contradictions that give it a mask of credibility. These texts present two mutually exclusive accounts of Judas' death: one states that he hanged himself (Matthew 27:5), while the other maintains that he bought a field, fell headlong, and burst open in the middle (Acts 1:18).


Psalm 41 connects directly with Psalm 118: both point to the time of the end. The righteous sinner is not an ethereally resurrected Jesus, but rather the collective righteous who, after reincarnating and falling into deception, are forgiven and purified when they understand the imperial falsehood. By turning away from the dogma, they pass through the gates of righteousness because they are the understanding ones who inherit the earth in this third millennium.

8	"Sandra and the Voice of the Devil. █


It all began in October 1996. We had just finished working on a group assignment at the house of a mutual friend. I was in love with her and, during the entire walk back, I tried to find the right moment to honestly tell her how I felt.


Instead of a conversation, however, I was met with unexpected hostility: she began treating me badly and insulting me for no logical reason.


The critical moment came when we reached the bus stop we both used in Zárate, San Juan de Lurigancho, on Malecón Checa Avenue. She remained cold and offensive, and I, confused and discouraged by her radical change in behavior, was about to leave. She bluntly said, 'There's your bus. Get on it and leave.' I hesitated. I still wanted to understand what was happening to her, suspecting that her erratic behavior might have been caused by something external, such as witchcraft. I stayed, waiting for an answer.


As soon as the bus drove away, the darkness of Malecón Checa filled with a real threat: about sixteen men who looked like criminals emerged from the banks of the Rímac River and walked straight toward us. I was terrified, trembling, yet I stepped in front of her, trying to protect her at all costs. At that moment of greatest danger, I heard the impossible: her mouth—the mouth of a woman I knew well—produced a deep, firm male voice, saying, 'I'm not afraid. I'm not afraid.'


https://www.youtube.com/embed/urHnEeGOAY8


When the men walked past us, I turned toward her, expecting to see relief or gratitude. I found nothing: not a word of thanks, nor the slightest sign of the terror that I myself was feeling. She simply stood there like a statue before boarding her own bus and leaving me behind, completely ignoring me.


Nine months later, after getting back in touch with her, she asked me to meet her to study. On one occasion, at the institute where we had met, when I finally tried to confront her by asking, 'Why did you treat me that way in 1996?' her tone suddenly changed. She began speaking like a little girl, shouting, 'Catch me if you can! Catch me if you can!' as she ran laughing out of the classroom.


I followed her upstairs until she reached a dead-end hallway. When I caught up with her, I found her leaning against the wall, sweating and trembling violently, without saying a word and appearing absolutely terrified. Stunned by what I was seeing, I decided to walk away, but she kept calling me over and over again, asking me by phone to keep looking for her.


Confused by the Catholic dogmas with which I had been indoctrinated since childhood, I could not understand that she was not a victim of demonic possession in need of my love or my prayers to be set free, but rather a cruel woman who used a cynical method to control me: for months she kept asking me to look for her again and again only to insult me, reject me, and eventually ambush me with criminals.

Some accounts suggest that Bahira may have spoken to him about aspects of the Christian religion, and that her influence may have contributed to the ideas that Muhammad later developed in the Qur'an.
